Stone Age

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Stone Age.

  3. According to the excavated evidence, the domestication of animals began in the Mesolithic period. The earliest pieces of evidence of domesti...PCS Mains ’061 paper

Stone Age: Core Facts and Sources

  • Stone Age
  • The period without documented evidence of its history is called the Prehistoric period . Proto-historic period is the period having shreds of evidence of a script which is still not deciphered. Proto-historic refers to the period between prehistoric and historical periods.
  • The time after which we get a documented description is called historic period .
  • The Stone Age comes under the Prehistoric period.
  • Indus valley civilization and Copper Age culture come under the Proto-historic period.
  • The historical period starts from around 6th century B.C.
  • In India, the research on Stone Age Civilization started at ﬁ rst in 1863. The whole Stone Age has been categorized into three parts on the basis of diﬀ erence of tools used. These are पुरापाषाण काल [Palaeolithic] period, मध्यपाषाण काल [Mesolithic] period and नवपाषाण काल [Neolithic] period.
  • पुरापाषाण काल [Palaeolithic] Period is further categorized into three periods on the same basis- (i) The Lower Palaeolithic period -Handaxe, Cleaver, Chopper, etc. (ii) The Middle Palaeolithic period- Points, Borer, Scrapper (iii) The Upper Palaeolithic period - Burins, Blades
  • Chopper-Chopping pebble pieces of tools were at first obtained from Sohan river valley of Punjab (Pakistan). At ﬁ rst, hand-axe pieces of equipment were found in Pallavaram and Attirampakkam near Chennai (Tamil Nadu). Other tools of this culture are cleaver, scraper, etc.
  • Robert Bruce Foote was a British geologist and archaeologist.

Indus Valley Civilization: Core Facts and Sources

  • सिंधु घाटी सभ्यता [Indus Valley] Civilization The Indus Valley Civilization is also known as the Harappa Civilization.
  • Archaeologists use the term 'culture' for a group of objects, distinctive in style, that are usually found togeth er within a speciﬁ c geographical area and at a period of time.
  • In the case of हड़प्पा सभ्यता [Harappan] culture, these distinctive objects include- seals, beads, weights, stone blades and even baked bricks.
  • The हड़प्पा सभ्यता [Harappan] seal is possibly the most distinctive artefact of the Harappan or सिंधु घाटी सभ्यता [Indus Valley] civilization. John Marshall, Director-General of the ASI, announced the discovery of a new civilization in the Indus Valley to the world in 1921.
  • Harappa is located in the Montgomery district (currently it is Sahiwal district) of Punjab province, Pakistan.
  • Mohenjodaro is located on the right bank of the Indus River in Larkana District of Sindh Province Pakistan.
  • According to Stuart Piggott, the two cities, Harappa and Mohenjodaro were “Twin capitals of one United Kingdom”.
  • Harappa is located at the left bank of Ravi River.
  • The carbon-14 (C 14) dating process indicates the हड़प्पा सभ्यता [Harappan] period to be from 2600 BC to 1700 BC which is highly accepted. During the period 2300 BC to 1900 B.C., the growth of this civilization was at its peak. Mesopotamia and Egyptian civilization are contemporary to the Indus civilization. The timeline of the diﬀ erent सिंधु घाटी सभ्यता [Indus Valley] civilization sites by various researchers – Researcher Timeline John Marshall (Mohenjodaro) Ernest Mackay (Mohenjodaro) Madho Sarup Vats (Harappa) C.J. Gadd (Trade with Mesapotamia) Mortimer Wheeler (Harappa) Walter A. Fairservis 3250 B.C. -2750 B.C. 2800 B.C. – 2500 B.C. 3500 B.C. – 2500 B.C. 2350 B.C. – 1700 B.C. 2500 B.C. – 1500 B.C. 2000 B.C. – 1500 B.C.
  • The हड़प्पा सभ्यता [Harappan] culture covered parts of Afghanistan, Punjab, Sindh, Baluchistan in Pakistan and in India Gujarat, Rajasthan, Haryana, the fringes of western Uttar Pradesh, J & K, Western Maharashtra. In India, It extended from Manda (Jammu and Kashmir) in the north to the Daimabad (Maharashtra) in the South, and from the Sutkagen Dor of Baluchistan in the West to Alamgirpur Meerut (Uttar Pradesh) in the east. The area formed a triangle and accounted for about 13 lakh square kilometers which is larger than Pakistan and certainly bigger than ancient Egypt and Mesopotamia.

Buddhism: Core Facts and Sources

  • Symbols of बौद्ध धर्म [Buddhism] related to the life of Buddha – Events Symbols Birth Lotus & Bull Great Departure (Mahabhinishkramana) Horse Enlightenment (Nirvana) Peepal (Bodhi tree) First Sermon (Dharamachakrapravartana) Wheel Death (Mahaparinirvana) Stupa
  • He spent the second, third, and fourth years of rainy season at the Rajgrih. Bimbisara, the ruler of मगध [Magadha] built a ‘Mahavihara’ called ‘Venuvana’ for his residence.
  • Travelling from Rajgriha, Buddha reached Vaishali, the capital of Lichchhavi where he spent the ﬁ fth year of the rainy season.
  • Lichchhivians built the famous ‘Kutagrasala’ in Mahavan for his residence.
  • Vaishali’s royal courtesan, Amrapali, became his disciple and provided his Amravatika for the residence of the Buddhist monks.
  • In the eighth year of Enlightenment, Gautama Buddha permitted women to enter as Bhikshuni into the Buddhist Sangha at Vaishali on the advice of his beloved disciple Ananda.
  • Buddha’s aunt (Mahaprajapati) who raised him as a mother was the ﬁ rst woman to enter the Buddhist Sangh.
  • Devadatta was the cousin of Buddha.
  • Devadatta ﬁ rst became a follower and then became an enemy of Buddha.
  • He wanted to remove Buddha from the Buddhist Sangh and become the head of the Sangh himself, but he did not succeed in it. The बौद्ध धर्म [Buddhism] was highly popularised in the Koshal state.

Gupta and Post-Gupta Period: Core Facts and Sources

  • गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] and Post-Gupta Period
  • गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] dynasty ruled during 275-550 AD. This dynasty was established by King Srigupta around 275 AD, but the ﬁ rst powerful ruler of Gupta dynasty was Chandragupta I who ruled during 319-335 AD . He held the title of ‘Maharajadhiraja’ (in contradiction to his predecessors) to show his importance.
  • गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] Samvat was started by Chandragupta-I in 319 A.D.
  • Historian V . A. Smith in his composition ‘The Early History of India’ called Samudragupta (335-375 A.D.) of गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] dynasty as ‘Napoleon of India’ for his great military conquests. The Allahabad (Prayag) stone/pillar inscription of Samudragupta, engraved on an सम्राट अशोक [Ashoka] pillar at Allahabad (Prayagraj) is associated with Samudragupta (335-375 AD). It is considered to be the most important historical document of the classical Gupta age in Sanskrit written by Harisena. It is also known as ‘Prayag Prashasti’.
  • The inscription is a panegyric (tribute) praising Samudragupta and lists the political and military achievements of his reign including his expeditions to the south. Built by सम्राट अशोक [Ashoka], originally it was situated in Kaushambi, which was later shifted to Allahabad (Prayagraj) by Akbar. The name of Jahangir and Birbal is also mentioned there.
  • Prayag Prashasti is on the सम्राट अशोक [Ashoka]n Pillar but has 4 diﬀ erent inscriptions: (1) Ashokan inscription in Brahmi script (2) Queen edict regarding the charitable deed of Ashoka’s wife, Karuvaki. (3) Samudragupta inscription written by Harisena in Sanskrit language and Brahmi script. (4) Jahangir inscription in Persian.
  • Another name of गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] ruler Chandragupta –II ‘Vikramaditya’ (375-415 AD) is found as Devgupta. Its evidence has been obtained from Sanchi and Vakataka inscriptions. Apart from the above-mentioned names, he is also known as Devraja (Sanchi inscription) and Devashri.
  • According to Mehrauli inscription the king Chandra was devoted to Vishnu and set up the Vishnu dhvaja (in honour of the God Vishnu) on a hill called Vishnupada.
  • ‘Mehrauli Iron Pillar Inscription’ acquired from Mehrauli in Delhi is located in the centre of the courtyard of Quwwat-ul-islam mosque near Qutub Minar at present.
  • गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire] ruler Chandragupta-II ‘Vikramaditya’ was known as the ‘Saka Conqueror’ as he admonished Saka power by defeating last Saka king of western India Rudrasimha-III. He adopted the title of ‘Sakari’ after this victory. He launched silver coins on this occasion. They were called ‘Rupaka’ in Gupta rule. The Nine Gems of Chandragupta II were (1) Kalidas (Poet), (2) Dhanvantari (Physician), (3) Varahamihira (Astronomer), (4) Amar Simha (Lexicographer), (5) Shanku (Architect) (6) Kshapanak (Astrologer), (7) Vararuchi (Grammarian), (8) Vetala Bhatta (Magician) (9) Ghata Karpara (Diplomat).

South India (Chola, Chalukya, Pallava and Sangam Period): Core Facts and Sources

  • South India (Chola, Chalukya, Pallava and संगम काल [Sangam Period] Period)
  • According to the संगम काल [Sangam Period] text, the terms Kon, Ko and Mannan were attributed to the King. Sangam is deﬁ ned as ‘Conference of Poets’ or ‘Forum of Scholars.’ Literature written by scholars during this conference, the oldest in south India, consisting of several collections or anthologies of poems, is mentioned as ‘Sangam Literature.’
  • Three संगम काल [Sangam Period]s were held under the patronage of Pandya kings in the south. Sangam Place President First Madurai Saint Agastya and T olkappiyar Second Kapadapuram Saint Agastya Third Madurai Nakkirara
  • Agastya was the sage who is credited with having Aryanised south India. He was born in Kashi, and it is believed that he travelled south from Kashi on request of Gods and later resided in the south. He is also known as “Father of Tamil Literature.” Tolkappiyam is not only the residual text of 2nd संगम काल [Sangam Period] era but also most ancient available text of Tamil literature. Author Tolkappiyar was one among the 12 disciples of Saint Agastya. It is a treatise on grammar and poetry. It has been written in formula style.
  • The author of Tamil Literature Silappadikaram was Ilango Adigal, second son of famous king Nedun-ceraladan of the Chera dynasty The epic narrates the tragic and poignant story of Kannagi and her husband, Kovalan.
  • Kural is considered as the Ethical bible of Tamil and Laghuveda of Tamil literature. It is also known as ‘Muppal’. It was written by the famous poet Tiruvalluvar.
  • Ramavataram, which is Tamil Ramayanam was authored by Kamban.
  • The history of early Cholas of संगम काल [Sangam Period] literature, is the interregnum between fall of Sangam Chola and the rise of Medieval Chola under Vijayalaya (850-871 AD.) in the 9 th century.
  • Chola dynasty was established by Vijayalaya-I in Southern India. Parantaka - I defeated Madura/Pandya king Rajasimha and assumed the title of “Madurai-Kond.”
  • Rajaraja-I defeated Cheras naval army in Kandalloor and his son Rajendra-I sent an expedition against Sailendra empire of South East Asia and conquered some of the areas as they were facing diﬃ culty in trade with China.

  2. When Muhammad-Bin-Qasim conquered SindhPCS Pre ’911 paper
  3. The ﬁ rst successful Muslim invader of India was Muhammad- Bin-Qasim. In the beginning of the 8th century A.D., under the leadership of Muha...Lower Sub ’021 paper

Muslim Invasion of India: Core Facts and Sources

  • Muslim Invasion of India
  • Mecca is situated in Saudi Arabia. The propounder of Islam religion, Hazarat Muhammad, was born here in 570 A.D. The place is a religious pilgrimage for Islamic followers. Muhammad died in 632 A.D.
  • ऋग्वेद [Rigveda] mentions the Bharatas as a tribe/people. The land name Bharat/Bharatavarsa is stated in later sanskrit texts (e.g. Vishnu Purana). According to tradition, the country was named after Dushyanta's son, 'Bharat'.
  • According to some scholars, the country is named after the name of Rishabhanath’s eldest son 'Bharat'.
  • Iranians addressed this country as ‘Hindustan’, and Greeks called it ‘India’. With respect to the people of Hind (Bharat), the word ‘Hindu’ was ﬁ rst used by Iranian/Persian.
  • The ﬁ rst successful Muslim invasion of India took place in 712 AD under the leadership of Arabian invader “Muhammad-Bin Qasim’. Earlier before 712 AD, a campaign was undertaken to India under the leadership of Ubaidullah, but he was defeated and killed in the encounter. After a great struggle Arabians conquered Sindh in 712 AD. Sindh was ruled by Dahir at that time. According to Persian texts ‘Chachnama’ we came to know about the conquest of Sindh by Arabians. Note: The year of the ﬁ rst successful invasion of India by Muslims is a subject of dispute among scholars. According to V .D. Mahajan, it is dated in 711 A.D. whereas according to Harishchandra Verma, it took place in 712 A.D.
  • The founder of Ghaznavid Empire was Alptigin. Ghaznavid dynasty is also known as Yamini dynasty. Alptigin made Ghazni his capital. In 998 AD, Mahmud became the ruler of Ghazni. According to Sir Henry Elliot, Mahmud invaded India 17 times. Mahmud Ghaznavi plundered Somnath Temple during the reign of Bhimdev I (1022-63).
  • Mahmud Ghaznavi, for the ﬁ rst time, invaded Chandelas in 1019-20 AD. At that time, the ruler of Chandela dynasty was Vidyadhar, who was the most powerful ruler of the dynasty. He was a Hindu ruler who had successfully resisted Muslim invasions. Between 1000-1027 AD, Mahmud Ghazni (Ghaznavi) attacked India 17 times, but the motive of these invasions was not to establish a stable Muslim rule in India but only to plunder the material assets. Baghdad’s Caliph Al- Qadir-Billah titled Mahmud Ghaznavi as ‘Yamin-ud-Daula’ and ‘Amin-ul-Millah’. II. Medieval History of India
  • Mahmud Ghaznavi patronised Persian learning. He respected scholars and artists alongwith famous scholars in Ghazni at that time. Utbi was his court historian. He wrote texts like Al-Kitab- al-Yamini/ ‘Tarikh-i-Yamini’. Other prominent members of his court were Albiruni, Baihaqi (writer of Tarikh-i-Subuktigin), Persian poet Uzari and Unsuri, Asjadi and Farrukhi.
  • The author of Shahnama is Firdausi. He was a famous court poet of Mahmud Ghaznavi. He earned the titled of ‘Homer of the East’. Firishta (1560-1620 A.D.) wrote books like Tarikh-i- Firishta, also Known as Gulshan-i- Ibrahimi. His full name was Muhammad Qasim Hindu Shah Astarabadi Firishta. His book ‘Tarikh-i-Firishta’ is dedicated to the ruler of Bijapur Ibrahim Adil Shah II. In the 11 th century, Al-Biruni came to India with Mahmud Ghazanavi. From his book ‘Kitab-ul-hind’ we come to know about the social and cultural conditions of contemporary India.

Muslim Invasion of India: Exam Focus and Inscriptions/Details

Delhi Sultanate: Literature: Core Facts and Sources

  • Arhitectural Style Associated Dynasty ‘Lotus bud’ fringe on the - खिलजी वंश [Khalji Dynasty]s underside of the arch Emergence of octagonal tomb - तुगलक वंश [Tughluq Dynasty]s Use of Bodegoi in the Pillar - विजयनगर साम्राज्य [Vijayanagara Empire] Massive entrance gate with - Sharqis inclined walls दिल्ली सल्तनत [Delhi Sultanate]: Literature
  • Abul Hassan Yaminuddin Khusrau, often called Amir Khusrau, was born in 1253 AD at Patiyali in present-day Kasganj district in Uttar Pradesh. Khusrau was called ‘Tuti- i - Hind’.
  • Some of his famous compositions were Miftah-ul-Futuh, Khazain- ul-Futuh (Tarikh-i-Alai), Ashika, Nuh Sipihr and तुगलक वंश [Tughluq Dynasty]- namah. He referred to himself as a 'Turkish-Indian'.
  • Amir Khusrau himself said that he knew, Sanskrit and other Indian languages, which are a part of Hindavi.
  • In India, Amir Khusrau was the originator of the early form of the musical genre known as Qawwali.
  • New Persian poetry style, ‘Sabk - i - Hindi’ or Hindustani style, was started by Amir Khusrau.
  • Musical instruments ‘Tabla’ and ‘Sitar’ were made prevalent in the 13 th/14th century by Amir Khusrau.
  • The development of Persian literature in India started with the advent of Muslims.
  • After the establishment of दिल्ली सल्तनत [Delhi Sultanate], the Persian language was granted the status of oﬃ cial language.
  • Tabakat-i-Nasiri is a treatise of Minhaj-us-Siraj (Minhaj- i-Siraj) dedicated to Sultan Nasiruddin Mahmud.

(d) The correct order of battles fought by Humayun is as follows- Devara (Daurah), Chausa, Kannauj and Sirhind. Humayun defeated Afghan rebels on the banks of Gomati at Devara or Daurah in 1532 A.D. On 17th May, 1540 AD Humayun was again defeated by Sher Shah in the Battle of Kanauj (Bilgram) and Humayun ﬂ ed from one place to the other after this war. Humayun occupied Delhi after the Battle of Sirhind on 22th June, 1555 AD.

Akbar

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Akbar.

UPPSC asks most here

  2. Diwan is a word in Persian vocabulary and was adopted by Muslims during the period of Umar Caliph. They used that term for the treasury depa...Lower Sub (Spl) ’03; Lower Sub (Spl) ’022 papers
  3. The famous Battle of Haldighati between Akbar and Maharana Pratap was fought in the year :RO/ARO Pre ’231 paper

Akbar: Core Facts and Sources

  • Elizabeth of England and Frederick the Great, during whose reign all were equal before the law.
  • Under the organised central administrative system in the reign of Akbar, Mir Bakshi was the head of military department but was not the chief commander of the army. Bakshi was responsible for recommending the recruitment and designation of proper rank and assignment of correct pay for soldiers.
  • He followed the practice of Dagh (branding of horses) in 1573-74 A.D.
  • Akbar organised soldiers by मनसबदारी व्यवस्था [Mansabdari System] System in 1574-75 A.D. The 'Mansab' means rank or position. 'Mansabdar' means the oﬃ cial who had rank or designation in the royal army or government oﬃ ce. The system was based on the decimal system.
  • Diwan is the word of Persian vocabulary and was adopted by Muslims in the period of Caliph Umar. They used that term for the treasury department. Muzaﬀ ar Khan Turbati was made Diwan (with Todar Mal as assistant) early in Akbar's reign. Todar Mal and Khwaja Shah Mansur later served in the ﬁ nance ministry & Akbar sometimes appointed more than one diwan. Diwan was a supreme oﬃ cial of ﬁ nancial aﬀ airs and revenue.
  • The मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] मनसबदारी व्यवस्था [Mansabdari System] system introduced by Akbar was taken from Central Asia. Genghis Khan's forces used a decimal troop organisation which inﬂ uenced the Akbar's mansabdari, which was a graded rank system.
  • मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] Emperor Akbar started the मनसबदारी व्यवस्था [Mansabdari System] system in the 19th year (1575 A.D.) of his reign. Mansab recipients were divided into three classes Mansabdar from 10 to less than 500, Amir from 500 to 2500 and Amir-i-Azam above 2500.
  • ज़ब्ती प्रणाली [Zabt System]i System was introduced for land revenue in the reign of Akbar, which was measurement based assessment; revenue circles (dasturs) with per-bigha cash rates; Ain-i-Dahsala (ten year average rates). Todarmal achieved fame in the ﬁ eld of land revenue.
  • Ain-i-Dahsala is a land revenue system introduced by Akbar in the 24th year of his reign (1580 A.D.) which is also known as Todarmal Bandobast. This system was instituted by Todarmal under Akbar. That is why it is known as Todarmal settlement. At that time, Todarmal was the ﬁ nance minister and his main assistant was Khwaja Shah Mansoor. Under this system, the average production of diﬀ erent crops, as well as the average prices prevailing over the last ten years was calculated. One- third of the average was ﬁ xed as a land revenue for state.
  • Akbar annexed Gujarat in 1572-73 AD and established a city named Fatehpur Sikri. A class of historians believes that Akbar built Buland Darwaza of Sikri as a victory tower to commemorate his win over Gujarat, while Percy Brown states its construction on the occasion of his south victory (1601 A.D.). Akbar launched “Tauhid-i-Ilahi” or “दीन-ए-इलाही [Din-i-Ilahi]” in 1582 A.D. Among Hindus, only Birbal accepted Din-i-Ilahi.

  • The ﬁ rst matrimonial alliance with the Rajputs was established by Akbar with Kachhwaha Rajput. He went on a pilgrimage to the Mausoleum of Shaikh Muinuddin Chishti at Ajmer in 1562 A.D. His route lai... UPPSC

  • Akbar supported Chishti sect expressing his faith in Suﬁ sm. He sought the company of the religious divine saints with whom he conversed with inborn zeal and humility. He frequently visited the Mausol... UPPSC
  • Akbar killed Adham Khan, the son of Maham Anga in 1562 AD as he assassinated Akbar’s prime minister, Atagh Khan. UPPSC
  • Mewar did not accept the supremacy of Akbar willingly. Rana Pratap of Mewar continued his struggle against Akbar for a long time. After the death of Rana Pratap, his son Rana Amar Singh signed a treat... UPPSC

Jahangir: Core Facts and Sources

  • visited the court of Jahangir. Sir Thomas Roe arrived in Surat on September, 1615 AD as an ambassador of British King James-I. He attended the court of Jahangir in Ajmer in January 1616 A.D. He got the opportunity to visit Mandu, Ahmedabad and Ajmer with the emperor. He also went for hunting with the emperor. He stayed for one year in Agra.
  • Peter Mundy was a English ईस्ट इंडिया कंपनी [East India Company] factor and traveller who came during the reign of Shah Jahan. Francisco Pelsaert was a Dutch merchant who came to India during Jahangir’s period and left a unique account of the मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] empire in his text “Jahangir's India : The Remonstrantie of Francisco Pelsaert” .
  • The मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] painting reached its zenith under the reign of Jahangir. Earlier, the paintings were concerned with the contents of manuscripts, but Jahangir freed it from this bondage.
  • Ustad Mansur and Abul Hasan were excellent painters of Jahangir’s reign. Emperor Jahangir bestowed both Ustad Mansur (Nadir-Ul-Asra) and Abul Hasan (Nadir-Ul-Zaman). Ustad Mansur was specialized in depicting plants and animals while Abul Hasan was a portrait artist.
  • Jahangir was an outstanding author as well as a critic. He wrote his autobiography in Persian language and titled it Tuzuk-i- Jahangiri.
  • Jahangir’s eldest son Khusrau revolted against Jahangir just after the coronation but the revolt was suppressed in 1606 A.D.
  • Shahzada Khurram revolted in 1622 A.D. and thereafter suﬀ ered defeat at Bilochpur in March 1623 under Mahabat Khan. The conﬂ ict ended with submission by 1625 A.D.
  • Mahabat Khan revolted against Jahangir in 1626 A.D.
  • The Mausoleums of मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] emperors Babur and Jahangir are located in Kabul and Shahdara (Lahore) respectively. Both of them are located in Afghanistan and Pakistan, respectively.
  • The tomb of Itmad-ud-Daula was built by Nur Jahan at Agra between 1622-1628 A.D. after the death of his father. This is the ﬁ rst tomb in India that is entirely made up of white marble. In spite of marble and inlay work, it was for the ﬁ rst time when a new style of ornamentation, Pietra dura, was used.

Chapter 32

Shah Jahan

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Shah Jahan.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. The Upanishadas were translated into the Persian language during the reign of Shah Jahan by his son Dara Shikoh as "Sirr-i-Akbar". Around 52...PCS Pre ’09; PCS Mains ’06; PCS Pre ’923 papers
  2. Who among the following deserves the credit for the construction of ‘Lal Qila’GIC ’10; PCS Pre ’022 papers
  3. The Upanishads were translated into Persian by Dara Shikoh under the title:Lower Sub (Spl) ’04; PCS Pre ’002 papers

Shah Jahan: Core Facts and Sources

  • Mahal, Moti Masjid (Agra), Khas Mahal, Musamman Burj, Nagina Masjid, Jama Masjid (Delhi), Taj Mahal and Red Fort.
  • Like Akbar’s Fatehpur Sikri’, Shah Jahan established a city named Shahjahanabad in his name in Delhi, and tried to equip it by building many beautiful and magniﬁ cent buildings. Among the buildings of Shahjahanabad, Red Fort is the main building. The name of the western entrance of this fort is Lahori Darwaza and the southern gate is Delhi Darwaza. It is unique in beauty and splendour.
  • Among the four sons of Shah Jahan, the eldest, Dara Shikoh was the most educated scholar and writer. He studied many Hindu texts and translated the Hindu texts like उपनिषद [Upanishad]s, Yoga Vashisht, Bhagwat Gita etc into Persian. The मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] emperor Shah Jahan abolished the Iranian Court tradition ‘Sijda’ started by Balban. Mughal Administration: Core Facts and Sources

  • smoking tobacco became so prevalent among people that to avoid this harmful habit, Jahangir prohibited it. He issued an order to ban it in 1617 A.D.
  • The मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] administration issued grants as Madad-i-Maash for the scholars and also to the religious persons. It was also called ‘Suyurghal’.
  • All the donated lands were inspected by ‘Sadra (Sadr-us-Sudur)’ and the Sadra also had the duty to keep an eye on the donation that they are not misused. These lands were not transferrable and the grantee possessed a right of inheritance over it. During the मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] period, the mint oﬃ cer was called Daroga. Gold, silver and copper coins were minted. Akbar issued the coins with ﬁ gures of Ram and Sita and 'Ram Siya' inscribed on them in Devanagari. The silver coin was called ‘Rupiya’. ‘Daam’ was copper coin largely created by Sher Shah (part of his tri-metallic system) and later continued under the Mughals.
  • Jahangir marked his ﬁ gure on his coins and named Nur Jahan, along with his name on some coins. Some of the coins were marked with his image holding cup of alcohol in hand.
  • The meaning of word ‘Batai’ during the medieval period was a system of crop sharing in which real produce was distributed between state and peasants.

Music and Paintings of Mughal Period: Core Facts and Sources

  • मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] era Shahab (Shihab) Canal was constructed by reinvigorating Firuz Shah's canal Rajabwah. In the time of Akbar, ﬁ rst Shihabuddin Khan & later Nuruddin Muhammad tar reconstructed the canal. During the time of Akbar, Governor of Delhi, Sihabuddin Khan, repaired the canal for irrigation purposes & changed its name to Shahab Canal. Music and Paintings of Mughal Period
  • War scenes, birds, animals and scenery were illustrated in मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] paintings. Humayun started the Mughal style o f painting. He met two distinguished painters, Khwaja Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali, during the years of his exile in Persia and Afghanistan. From these artists Humayun and his son Akbar learned lessons in drawing and painting.
  • Akbar got a number of texts painted. The ﬁ rst major project was ‘Dastan-i-Amir Hamza’. Almost 100 painters took part in its preparation, ﬁ rst under the supervision of Mir Sayyid Ali and later, under Abdus Samad. There were about 1400 paintings in it. This book mentioned the heroic work of the Persian hero, Amir Hamza (uncle of Prophet Muhammad).
  • The art of मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] painting reached great heights during Jahangir’s reign. Jahangir was a highly skilled connoisseur of painting. Abul Hasan (the title of Nadiru-z-Zaman), Ustad Mansur (Nadir-ul-Asra), Faruq Begh, Bishan Das, Aqa Reza, Muhammad Nadir, Muhammad Murad, Manohar, Madhav, Govardhan, etc were the main artists of Jahangir's court. Abul Hassan made a painting of Jahangir‘s coronation. This painting is inscribed in the Jahangir’s autobiography ‘Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri’ as frontispiece. Thus, Jahangir’s period was called ‘Golden Age of Mughal Paintings'.
  • ‘Pahari School’, ’Rajput School’, ‘मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] School’ are diﬀ erent styles of medieval painting.
  • Aurangzeb banned the music as he found it as anti-Islamic, but during the same period, most books on Indian classical music were written in the Persian language. Despite being anti-music. Aurangzeb was a skilled instrumentalist who used to play the Indian instrument called ‘Veena'.
  • ‘Todi’ Raga is used to sing early in the morning. It was sung by Bhats and Charans in the emperor’s court.
  • During the reign of Akbar, Tansen and Swami Haridas were prominent Dhrupad singers. Tansen was a famous musician of Akbar‘s court. Tansen‘s original name was Ramtanu Pandey. He was one of the Akbar’s Navratanas. Before coming to the court of Akbar, Tansen was in the court of King Ramchandra of Rewa from where Akbar summoned him to his court and gave a very high post.
  • Akbar had given Tansen the title of ‘Kanthabharnvanivilas’.
  • Vilas Khan was the musician of Jahangir’s court. The prominent singers of the court of Shah Jahan were Jagannath, Ramdas, Sukhsen, Sursen, Lal Khan, Durrang Khan, etc. Khyal singing became popular in the time of Muhammad Shah ‘Rangila’.

Music and Paintings of Mughal Period: Exam Focus and Inscriptions/Details

Sikh Sect: Core Facts and Sources

  • (d) None of the above Chhattisgarh P.C.S. (Pre) 2023 Ans. (c) A hugely interesting feature of medieval India was the 'Sarais' which ringed cities and dotted the vast space of the Indian sub-continent. Sarais were largely built on a simple square or rectangular plain and were meant to provide temporary accommodation to Indian and Foreign travellers, pilgrims, merchants, traders, etc. In eﬀ ect, sarais were public domains which thronged with people of varied cultural backgrounds. This led to cross-cultural interaction, inﬂ uences and syncretic tendencies in the cultural mores of the times and at the level of the people.

Chapter 41

Advent of European Companies

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Advent of European Companies.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. French East India Company was constituted during the reign of-PCS Mains ’14; Lower Sub ’132 papers
  2. Who among the following had welcomed Vasco da Gama at Calicut?Lower Sub ’131 paper
  3. Who among the following visitors to India did not belong to Portuguese country? (a) Duarte Barbosa (b) Domingo Paes (c) Peter Mundy (d) Fern...RO/ARO Pre ’211 paper

Advent of European Companies: Core Facts and Sources

  • III. Modern History of India The Advent of European Companies
  • According to one theory, Vasco da Gama reached India with the help of an Arab navigator Ahmad Ibn Majid, but according to historical & authentic evidence, the person who helped Vasco da Gama reach Indian port of Calicut was a Gujarati sailor. Vasco da Gama sailed to the Western Coast of India, at the port of Calicut on May 20, 1498. He had successfully discovered a new and alternative sea route to India from Europe. Zamorin (Zamorin was the ancestral title of the rulers of Calicut), then the ruler of Calicut, welcomed Vasco da Gama. The arrival of Vasco da Gama in India started a new era of trade between Portuguese and Indians. Vasco da Gama returned with the cargo having 60 times more proﬁ t than the expedition cost, in the business of Black Pepper which was enough to lure other merchants to India.
  • In 1505 A.D., Francisco de Almeida arrived in India as ﬁ rst Portuguese Viceroy . In the light of Portuguese trade interests and to establish Portuguese authority in the Indian Ocean, Almeida rendered the Blue Water Policy .
  • Afonso de Albuquerque came as the Governor of the Portuguese region in India 1509 A.D. after Almeida. In 1510 A.D., he snatched Goa from Adil-Shahi Sultanate, the rulers of Bijapur, and established his authority there. He is known as the real founder of the Portuguese power in India.
  • Albuquerque in 1511 A.D. captured Malacca, an important South-East Asian Market and, in 1515, captured Hormuz situated at the mouth of Persian Gulf.
  • Nuno da Cunha assumed the Governor ofﬁ ce after Lopo Vaz de Sampaio in 1529 A.D. and remained the Governor until the end of 1538 A.D.
  • A new sea route to India was discovered by Portuguese traveller Vasco da Gama whereas Columbus, a traveller originally from Italy and ﬁ nanced by Spain discovered America, and Tasman of Holland discovered Van Diemen's Land (currently Tasmania) and New Zealand. The ﬁ rst European to reach Australia was English Navigator Willem Janszoon (1712-1780), while the British Navigator Captain Cook arrived at Australia in 1770 A.D. (about 164 years later, when Willem Janszoon reached to Australia). Captain Cook is credited with surveying Australia’s east coast and laying foundation of British rule there.
  • In 1503, at Cochin, ﬁ rst Portuguese Fort ( ﬁ rst European Fort in India) was constructed by Afonso de Albuquerque (during his ﬁ rst visit to India in 1503 A.D.). The fort was named as ‘Emmanuel Fort’.
  • The sequence of Arrival of European Companies to India is : Portuguese, Dutch, English, Danish and French.
  • Portuguese were the ﬁ rst to arrive in 1498 A.D. and were last to leave India in 1961 (de Jure transfer took place in August 1962).

  • Naval superiority of the British. Select the correct answer from the codes given below. Codes: (a) Only 1 (b) Only 2 (c) Both 1 and 2 (d) Neither 1 nor 2 U.P.R.O. / A.R.O. (Pre) (Re-Exam) 2016 Ans (c) There were many causes of the success of the British in India. Naval superiority, getting huge wealth and manpower from Bengal, better internal coordination among British army etc. were few of them. ईस्ट इंडिया कंपनी [East India Company] and Nawabs of Bengal *Murshid Quli Khan was the last Governor of Bengal who was independently appointed by मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] King. Murshid Quli Khan shifted his Capital to Maksudabad from Dhaka and named it Murshidabad. He started Izara System in the land settlement. * Alivardi Khan (1740-1756 A.D.) comparing Europeans with the Honeybees, said that if they are left undisturbed, they will give you honey, and if try to disturb them they will bite you till death. *After the death of Alivardi Khan, his grandson Siraj-ud-Daula succeeded to the throne of Bengal. The Black Hole tragedy took place during his reign on 20 th June 1756 A.D. According to one of the 23 survivors, J.Z. Holwell, 146 English prisoners were put in a cell with a dimension of 18 feet in length and 14 feet 10 inches in breadth. 123 of them died due to suffocation and only 23 survived. This incident is known as Black Hole tragedy. Contemporary Muslim historian Ghulam Hussain in his book ‘Siyar-ul- Mutakherin’ has not mentioned this incident. *Robert Clive is known as the founder of the English Empire in India who defeated the forces of Bengal’s Nawab Siraj-ud-Daula in the Battle of Plassey (presently known as Palasi) on 23 rd June, 1757 A.D. and sown the seeds of British rule in India. The Battleﬁ eld of Plassey is situated on the Banks of Bhagirathi River in Nadia district of West Bengal. Robert Clive was the Governor in 1758- 60 A.D. and 1765-67 A.D. in India. During this period he signed Allahabad Treaty with Nawab of Awadh Shuja-ud-Daula and Mughal Emperor Shah Alam II During his governorship White Mutiny broke out among English soldiers.* Plundering of resources of Bengal by the British Company was started during Clive’s governorship. *Mir Qasim (1760-63 A.D.) was the ablest among the successors of Alivardi Khan. As a ‘Faujdar’ of Poornia and Rangpur, Mir Qasim had already proven his ability. To save his courtiers from repeated conspiracies by Britishers and to avoid interference from Englishmen, Mir Qasim shifted his Capital to Munger from Murshidabad so as to stay away from Calcutta. He consolidated his army using European methods. He made arrangements in Munger for the manufacturing of Matchlock guns. Mir Qasim also took some brave steps so as to improve the economic conditions in Bengal. The of ﬁ cers involved in embezzlement were ﬁ ned heavily, some new taxes were introduced *The Combined forces of Mir Qasim, Shuja-ud-Daula (Nawab of Awadh), and Mughal King Shah Alam II were defeated by Britishers in the Battle of Buxar on 22/23 October, 1764 AD. English army was led by Hector Munro. Henry Vansittart was the then Governor of Bengal. This victory of Britishers made the English a great power in northern India and contenders for supremacy over the whole country. The area up to Allahabad was now under the British and the door of Delhi was now open for British. Battle of Buxar changed the course of Indian History. During this battle, Mir Jafar was Nawab of Bengal and Delhi was ruled by Shah Alam II. By the treaties of Allahabad (August, 1765) Shah Alam II agreed to reside at Allahabad, under the company’s protection. Nawab Shuja-ud-Daula surrendered Allahabad and Kora to Emperor Shah Alam II. *Shah Alam II issued a decree granting Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa to the East India Company. *Robert Clive became the governor of Bengal and started Dual government system in Bengal. *The Company appointed two deputy Diwans, Mohammad Raza Khan for Bengal and Raja Shitab Roy for Bihar to exercise Diwani functions. Rohilla Sardar Ghulam Qadir annexed Delhi in July 1788, and August 1788, he ousted Shah Alam II and made him blind. During the time of Shah Alam II in 1803, Delhi fell completely to East India Company. Shah Alam II and his two successors Akbar II (1806-37 AD) and Bahadur Shah II (1837-57 AD) were mere pensioners of the East India Company. *The British came to Sylhet (then Sylhet was part of Bengal Presidency) in 1765 AD after acquiring the Diwani right of Bengal. After the War of Burma, David Scott tried to connect Assam (Brahmaputra Valley) with Sylhet through roads to Jaintia and Garo Hill areas, but faced a very tough resistance from the Khasi tribes who lived in these areas under the leadership of Tirot Sing. *K.M. Panikar said that from 1757 to 1773 AD the Company made Bengal a ‘State of Robbers’. → Option A. UPPSC

Chapter 44

Governor/Governor General/Viceroy

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Governor/Governor General/Viceroy.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Lord Dalhousie efﬁ ciently made the plan and accused Nawab of Awadh, Wajid Ali Shah for maladministration and merged his state into the Brit...PCS Pre ’94; PCS Pre ’91; PCS Pre ’903 papers
  2. Slavery was abolished by Act-V of 1843 by the then Governor-General of India Lord Ellenborough. His tenure was from 1842 to 1844 AD.PCS Mains ’11; UDA/LDA Mains ’10; PCS Pre (Spl) ’083 papers
  3. Oudh was amalgamated into the British dominion in the year–UDA/LDA Pre ’13; PCS Mains ’102 papers

Governor/Governor General/Viceroy: Core Facts and Sources

  • In the Third Battle of Panipat (1761 A.D.), Ahmad Shah Abdali defeated the मराठा साम्राज्य [Maratha Empire]s. Tipu Sultan was killed in the Fourth Anglo-Mysore War in 1799 A.D. During the war of Plassey (1757 A.D.), Mir Jafar conspired against Nawab Siraj-ud-Daulah supporting Britishers. Governor/Governor General/ Viceroy
  • Both Civilian and Military reforms took place during the period of Robert Clive. Under the civilian reform, a ban was imposed on the gift to of ﬁ cials and controlled private trade (Clive imposed conditioned ban on accepting gifts; i.e. for accepting gifts above Rs. 1000 of value, a pre-permission of governor is necessary). Under the Military reforms, he ended ‘double batta’ for European ofﬁ cers and from January 1, 1766 AD this allowance was only granted to soldiers who served outside the boundaries of Bengal and Bihar (except Allahabad).
  • Under administrative reforms, Warren Hastings in 1772 A.D. ended the dual government system of Bengal by order of the Court of Directors. He transferred the State’s treasury from Murshidabad to Calcutta. Warren Hastings believed that the whole land belonged to the ruler. To reform in Revenue ﬁ eld, he chose trial and error method . Warren Hastings in 1774 A.D. was made Governor-General of Bengal by the Regulating Act 1773. The act provides for Governor-General and four consellors (“Council of four”), total ﬁ ve (including Governor-general). The Governor General was head of the government and had casting vote if equally divided. It was established to look after the affairs of Bengal. The Chairman of the Executive Council was called Governor-General. Several types of reforms in Police, Judiciary, Taxation, Faujdari related Acts etc. took place during the time of Cornwallis.
  • Asiatic Society of Bengal was established on 15 th January, 1784 A.D. in the tenure of Governor General Warren Hastings.
  • Warren Hastings and Wellesley were related to the Ring-Fence theory.
  • Impeachment proceedings were initiated on Hastings for injustices and atrocities committed by him but in the light of his service, proceedings which took place between the years 1788 A.D. and 1795 A.D. (until he was acquitted in April 1795) The following were the policies of Britishers towards Indian States (Princely States):- 1- Company’s struggle for equality with Indian provinces (1740-1765 AD) 2- Policy of Ring Fence (1765-1813 AD) 3- Policy of Subordinate Isolation (1813-1857 AD) 4- Policy of Subordinate Union (1858-1935 AD) 5- Policy of Equal Federation (1935-1947 AD)
  • Britishers developed a new system for justice delivery through hierarchical institutions consisting of Diwani and Faujdari Courts. This system was started by Warren Hastings but Cornwallis made it more rigid. He concentrated all powers in the hands of Collector. In 1787 A.D. the District Collector was made the Diwani Judge of Diwani Courts and was granted some Faujdari Powers to deliver Justice in some Faujdari cases too.
  • In 1790-92 AD, District Faujdari Courts with Indian Judges were abolished and in their place four Circuit Courts, three for Bengal and one for Bihar, were appointed. These Courts were headed by European of ﬁ cials and the Justice was delivered with the help of Indian Kazis and Muftis. Cornwallis initiated his rule of speciﬁ cation of law, it was a set of regulations that codiﬁ ed administrative, revenue and judicial reforms of ईस्ट इंडिया कंपनी [East India Company] in Bengal.
  • ‘Cornwallis Code’ is the collection of judicial reforms by Cornwallis in 1793 A.D. This reform was based on the law of ‘Separation of Powers’. He separated Revenue and Judicial administration. The judicial and faujdari power of collector was taken back and he was entrusted with powers related to taxation only.
  • A new hierarchy of ofﬁ cers was created for jobs in the District Diwani Courts. District Judges were given works of Faujdari and Police too. The modern Indian Civil Services was reorganised and professionalised by Cornwallis (1793). He forbade private trade and gifts, raised salaries, separated functions and gave legal form to civil service rules; he was regarded as the reformer of civil service. Lord Cornwallis was the Governor General of Bengal from 1786 to 1793 and from 30 th July, 1805 A.D. to 5 th October, 1805 A.D. He died at Ghazipur (Uttar Pradesh) on 5 th October 1805 A.D. His grave is present in Ghazipur.

  • Given below are two statements, one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other as Reason (R) : Assertion (A) : The British Government introduced different land revenue system in different part of India. Mahalwari (around 30% of the area) system around was in U.P, Central Province, Punjab. Hence, it is clear that because of this, different classes were created in Indian peasantry. Hence, option (a) is correct. → Option A. UPPSC

Chapter 46

Revolt of 1857

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Revolt of 1857.

UPPSC asks most here

  1. The ﬁ rst War of Independence was started from Meerut. Sepoys refused to use the new ri ﬂ e cartridges and revolted openly. They left Meerut...PCS Pre ’94; PCS Pre ’902 papers
  2. The real name of which one of the following leaders of 1857 was ‘Ram Chandra Pandurang’?PCS Pre ’11; UDA/LDA Pre ’102 papers
  3. Mangal Pandey was a sepoy of 34th Bengal Native Infantry regiment.PCS Pre ’141 paper

Revolt of 1857: Core Facts and Sources

  • Lapse. Dalhousie by Doctrine of lapse, annexed Jhansi to British Empire in 1854.
  • In June, 1857, the revolt began in Jhansi under the leadership of Rani Laxmi Bai (the revolt in Jhansi was outbreaked/began by sepoys Laxmibai Later assumed control and led resistance). She died on 17/18 June, 1858 A.D. ﬁ ghting General Hugh Henry Rose . On her death, Hugh Rose said, “Among the Indian revolutionaries the women lying here is the only man.”
  • Revolt in Lucknow began under the leadership of Begum Hazrat Mahal on 30 th May, 1857 A.D. She declared her minor son Birjis Qadr as Nawab and took to all affairs of the Lucknow into herself. On 21 st March, 1858 A.D., Campbell with the help of Gurkha Regiment regained Lucknow.
  • On 5 June 1857, the rebel repoys halted at Kalyanpur; on 6 June they returned to Kanpur, where Nana Saheb (Nana Dhondhu Pant) assumed their leadership. He was later proclaimed/declared Peshwa at Kanpur (on June 30, 1857 as recorded by R.C. Majumdar) and the war of Independence spread in Kanpur. He was helped a lot by Commander-in-Chief (his General Military Advisor) Tatya Tope, The original name of Tatya Tope was Ramchandra Pandurang Tope . After being betrayed by a close friend Man Singh, he was captured when he was camping in a forest. Tope was captured on 7 April 1859, after a hurried trial the military court gave the verdict on 15th April 1859 punishing him with death by hanging and he was hanged on 18th April 1859 at Sipri/Shivpuri.
  • Kunwar Singh led the revolt from Jagdishpur (currently Bhojpur district), Bihar. He was from Shahabad region of Bihar.
  • Maniram Dutt Deewan in Assam declared Kandarpeswar Singha, the grandson of the last king as the king and planned an armed insurrection conspiracy. But soon after Maniram Deewan was caught and hanged for his promotional activities regarding the revolt.
  • Thakur Kushal Singh of Auwa defeated the combined army of English and Jodhpur.
  • Maulavi Ahmadullah Shah provided leadership to the revolt from Faizabad (Ayodhya) in 1857 A.D. He was a determined opponent of British rule. He was native to Chinapattan of Madras, but he migrated to Faizabad. He asked all the different religious believers to stand as one against these English foreigners and to wipe them out from India. About him, Britishers said a man of great abilities, of undaunted courage of stern determination; and best soldier among the rebels. A bounty of Rs. 50,000 was placed on his arrest (dead or alive).
  • Mirza Ghalib saw the revolt of 1857 A.D. with his own eyes. He was born on 27 th December, 1797 AD in Agra and died in Delhi on 15 th February, 1869.
  • Shindhiyas of Gwalior helped the Britishers in the suppression of the Revolt.

Development of Education in Modern India: Core Facts and Sources

Chapter 49

Development of Press in Modern India

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Development of Press in Modern India.

UPPSC asks most here

Development of Press in Modern India: Core Facts and Sources

  • Development of Press in Modern India
  • James Augustus Hicky in 1780 A.D. started the Bengal Gazette, the ﬁ rst newspaper in India. Lord Wellesley enacted Censorship of Press Regulations, 1799 . It imposed almost wartime press restrictions including pre-censorship. In 1807 this Act included pamphlets, books and magazines too.
  • Lord Hastings in 1818 abolished the strict pre-censorship of the press in India and dispensed with their submitting their papers to an ofﬁ cer of government previous to publications. Vernacular Press Act, 1878 was passed during the tenure of Lord Lytton (1876-1880 A.D.). The Act empowered the district magistrate to call upon the printer and publisher of any vernacular newspaper to enter into a bond with the government not to cause disaffection against the government or antipathy between persons of different religion, caste, race, through published material.
  • The press and publisher could also be required to deposit security which could be forfeited if the regulation were violated, and press equipment could be seized if the offence reoccurred.
  • Under this Act, proceedings were instituted against Som Prakash, Bharat Mihir, Dacca Prakash and Samachar.
  • B. G. Tilak was imprisoned for anti-British journalism in 1882. He was the ﬁ rst Indian to be punished for the performance of his duty in this ﬁ eld.
  • Tilak was arrested after the murder of W.C. Rand and ofﬁ cer Charles Ayerst on the grounds of the publication of a poem, “Shivaji’s utterances” in Kesari, and a speech that Tilak had delivered at the Shivaji festival, justifying Afzal’s Khan murder by Shivaji. Tilak’s defence of Shivaji’s killing of Afzal Khan was portrayed by the prosecution as an incitement to kill British ofﬁ cials. In 1897, he was imprisoned for 18 months for inciting Chapekar brothers for killing W.C. Rand and Charles Ayerst through his articles published in Kesari (he was charged for sedition for writing such articles).
  • 'The Free Hindustan' newspaper was started by Tarak Nath Das in North America (Vancouver).
  • Raja Ram Mohan Roy used the press as a medium to express and publish his views. In 1821 he published ‘Sambad Kaumudi’ Bengali weekly newspaper. After one year he published a newspaper ‘Mirat- ul-Akhbar’ in the Persian language. ‘ Indian Mirror’ was published from Calcutta (Bengal). Credit for establishment of Indian Mirror (1861) goes to Debendranath Tagore and Manmohan Ghosh.
  • Lala Hardayal, Sohan Singh Bhakana and Kartar Singh Sarabha, etc. founded Hindustan Ghadar Party (Ghadar Party) in USA and Canada to free India from British Rule. ‘Ghadar’ a weekly publication, was a manifesto of the party which was ﬁ rst published on 1st November, 1913 at San Francisco in Urdu. From 9 th December, 1913 A.D. its printing in Gurmukhi script also started. It was later on published in Marathi, Hindi, English and Gujarati. Its one edition was also published in Pashto language.

Development of Press in Modern India: Exam Focus and Inscriptions/Details

  • Raja Ram Mohan Roy, to promote and circulate his thoughts, published the ‘Sambad Kaumudi’ weekly newspaper in 1821 A.D. and a Persian newspaper Mirat-ul-Akhbar in 1822 A.D. He knew English, Bengali, P... UPPSC

Social Religious Movement: Core Facts and Sources

  • (b) The New York Times (c) The Times of India (d) More than one of the above 70th B.P.S.C. Re-Exam 2024 Ans. (a) The article mentioned, regarding the potential future role of the भारतीय राष्ट्रीय कांग्रेस [Indian National Congress], was written in the newspaper The Indian Mirror in its January 1886 edition. Socio-Religious Movements
  • The dawn of the nineteenth century witnessed the birth of a new vision, a modern vision, among some enlightened sections of the Indian society. This enlightened vision was to shape the course of events for decades to come and even beyond. This process of reawakening, sometimes but not with full justiﬁ cation de ﬁ ned as ‘Renaissance’, did not always follow the intended line and gave rise to some undesirable by-products as well, which have become as much a part of daily existence in the whole of the Indian subcontinent as the fruits of these reform movements.
  • Raja Ram Mohan Roy often called the Father of Indian Renaissance is also known by names like ‘Prophet of Indian Nationalism’, Father of Modern India, ‘First Modern Man’ and ‘Yugdoot’. In 1815 A.D. he set up the Atmiya Sabha in Calcutta to propagate Monotheistic ideals of Hindu religion among his youth followers (mostly rich and influential). In 1828 A.D. he founded Brahmo Sabha, which was later renamed Brahmo Samaj .
  • Devendranath Tagore founded Tattvabodhini Sabha to propagate rational thinking, study ancient texts (like उपनिषद [Upanishad]) and spread the ideals of Rajaram Mohan Roy in 1839 A.D. Brahmo Samaj was the ﬁ rst reform movement in the Hindu religion which was in ﬂ uenced by modern western liberal ideas and a new reading of ancient Indian texts.
  • मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] Emperor Akbar II gave Raja Ram Mohan Roy the title of Raja and sent him to the court of British King William IV as his ambassador in 1830. Roy was sent there to discuss about the increment in the pension of Akbar II. On 27th September, 1833 Raja Ram Mohan Roy died at Bristol in England. The mausoleum of Roy is present at Bristol.
  • Raja Ram Mohan Roy advocated English Education in India. According to him, a moderate Western education is necessary to pull us out of the darkness and to provide Indians with partnership in the administration of the country. He opposed idol worship, Child marriage, practice of Sati etc. evil social practices of India.
  • Ramakrishna Paramahamsa emphasized that Hari, Ram, Allah, and Christ are different names for the same God and that there are many ways to God and s alvation.
  • Swami Vivekananda spread Ramakrishna’s message and tried to reconcile it to needs of contemporary Indian Society. He emerged as the preacher of Neo-Hinduism. At the Parliament of the World's Religions held at Chicago in 1893, Swami Vivekananda delivered his famous speech. Subhash Chandra Bose said, “So far as Bengal is concerned Vivekananda may be regarded as the spiritual father of Modern nationalist movement.” Swami Vivekananda founded Ram Krishna Mission in 1897 A.D. (it was registered of ﬁ cially in 1909 A.D. under Societies Registration Act 1860). Its headquarter was opened at Belur in Howrah (West Bengal). The objective of Ram Krishna Mission was non-political, non-sectarian spiritual movement, engaged in various forms of humanitarian, social and cultural service activity, but it was highly inspired by the Ancient culture of India. The Theory of Pure Vedanta is the ideal of this mission. Its main aim is the highest spiritual upliftment of Human’s inner self. It also accepted traditional practices of Hindu religion in their original form such as idol worship, rituals, symbols, etc. The second feature of the mission is : ‘Belief in the truth of all religions’. Swami Vivekananda said that all religious beliefs are different ways to reach the same destination that is one God.
  • Mulshankar or Dayanand Saraswati was born in the old Morvi Princely State, Kathiyawara in Gujarat in a Brahmin family. From 1845-60 (for fifteen years) he wandered to different places in search of knowledge. In 1860 he came under swami Virajananda Saraswati at Mathura and started preaching in 1864. In November, 1869, he engaged in a mighty Shastrartha with leaders of Hindu orthodoxy at Kashi. In 1867 A.D., he hoisted “Pakhand Khandini Pataka”. In 1875 A.D., he founded ‘Arya Samaj’ in Bombay. His views are inscribed in his famous book Satyartha Prakash. ऋग्वेद [Rigveda]dibhashya- bhumika, Rigveda Bhashya, Advaitmat Khandan, Panch Mahayajya Vidhi and Ved-Viruddh-mat-Khandanam were important works of Dayanand Saraswati. The Arya Samaj was spreading rapidly as a reaction against the western inﬂ uences. In 1877 A.D., its headquarter was shifted to Lahore which helped in accelerating the spread of this movement. Dayananda’s slogan of “Go Back to Vedas” was a call for a revival of वैदिक काल [Vedic period] learning and purity of religion. He rejected the religious practices like idol worship, incarnations, tantra-mantra, etc. His aim was the oneness of all religions, society, and of the nation itself. He is also called as “The (Martin) Luther of India” for his efforts in religious reforms. Arya Samaj got divided into two groups, one supported western education and other one opposed western education. Swami Sraddhanand (Mahatma Munshi Ram Vij), Lekhraj etc. were opposed to western education. They established ‘Gurukul’ in 1902. Lala Lajpat Rai and Hansraj were supporters of western education. They established Dayanand Anglo Vedic College. Dayanand Saraswati was the ﬁ rst to use word Swaraj and advocated Hindi as the of ﬁ cial language of India. He was the ﬁ rst to suggest renunciation of foreign goods and the adoption of country-made indigenous goods.
  • After being inspired by Keshab Chandra Sen , Atmaram Pandurang founded Prarthana Samaj in 1867 A.D.

Moderates and Extremists in Congress: Core Facts and Sources

  • Ans. (c) “Jana Gana Mana” is the national anthem of India. Originally written in Bengali, it is the ﬁ rst of ﬁ ve stanzas of a Brahmo hymn composed and scored by Nobel laureate Rabindranath Tagore. Morley-Minto Reforms: Core Facts and Sources

  • the Muslim members of the council were not elected by general electorate but were elected by the Muslim voters only. This system accepted Muslims as a separate community in India. This created many fundamental issues in India. The unity of India was attacked and demolished in just a stroke. Gandhiji said - " The Morley – Minto Reforms have been our undoing. Had it not been for the separate electorates then established then we should have settled our differences by now."

  • In December 1911, a grand Delhi Durbar was organized to welcome British King George V and Queen Mary. However, it was the Calcutta High Court which exercised jurisdiction over the said new province. The Bengal got the identity of a new province in 1912 (after East and West Bengal was reunited). Assam was restored to its previous position of 1874.

Peasant Movement and Kisan Sabha

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Peasant Movement and Kisan Sabha.

Peasant Movement and Kisan Sabha: Core Facts and Sources

  • I.A.S. (Pre) 2011 Ans. (a) In Kheda, Gujarat, the peasants were frequently plagued by poverty, famine, and British discrimination. The famine had destroyed the agrarian economy of the region. The revolt was against the taxes and rents that had to be paid to Britishers. The Government said that if the taxes were not paid, the property would be seized. Hence, statement 1 is right while 2 is wrong. Peasant Movement and Kisan Sabha
  • Workers of होम रूल आंदोलन [Home Rule Movement] League were very active in Awadh. They started to organize Peasants. Organization was named as 'Kisan Sabha'.
  • U.P. Kisan Sabha was formed in February 1918 A.D. by Indra Narayan Dwivedi , Gauri Shankar Mishra with the support of Madan Mohan Malaviya. The organizations organized peasants on a large scale.
  • These organizations spread awareness amongst the farmers on such a large scale that in 1918 A.D. Delhi Session of Congress a huge number of farmers participated from U.P.
  • In Pratapgarh District of Oudh ‘Nai-Dhobi bandh’ social boycott movement was started against the exploiting landlords.
  • Jhinguri Singh and Durgapal played an important role in this movement, but later on, a new face, Baba Ramchandra emerged who operated the movement wisely and made it strong and combative. In mid 1920 A.D., he emerged as a peasant’s leader and started organizing peasants in Oudh. He had extraordinary organizational capability. In 1920 A.D., with his efforts, Oudh Kisan Sabha was established.
  • Swami Sahajanand Saraswati was the founder of Bihar Provincial Kisan Sabha (BPKS) in 1929. In his Agricultural Reform programme, the main aim was the eradication of Zamindari system and to bring ownership rights to the farmers. For his devoted services to farmers he is also called as ‘Kisan-Pran’.
  • On April 11, 1936. All India Kisan Congress was formed in Lucknow. Swami Sahajanand became its ﬁ rst President and N.G. Ranga became ﬁ rst General Secretary.
  • The Kisan Congress was later renamed as 'All India Kisan Sabha' (AIKS) held its session at the same time of the Congress session in Faizpur which was chaired by N.G. Ranga .
  • Eka Movement (1921-22 A.D.) was led by Madari Pasi of a lower class family. The main centres of this movement were Hardoi, Barabanki, Bahraich and Sitapur. Main complains of farmers included the increment in rent and the system of collection of rent in produce. Rent was 50% higher than the recorded rent generally. In this movement, people didn’t refuse to pay rent and insisted on collecting the revenue in cash because of high inﬂ ation.

  2. The ﬁ rst session of All India Trade Union Congress held at Bombay in 1920 was presided over by :PCS Mains ’11; PCS Mains ’062 papers
  3. Kanpur Conspiracy Case was against the Communists who were abhorred by the British Government. Some newly turned communists named M. N. Roy,...GIC ’10; PCS Pre ’012 papers

Trade Union and Communist Party: Core Facts and Sources

  • Trade Union and Communist Party
  • Madras Labour Union was formed in 1918 A.D. under the leadership of B.P. Wadia and V . Kalyanasundaram and it became the First modern labour Union. Buckingham and Carnatic Clothe Mills labourers were part of this union. Soon, two Labour Unions in Bombay, one at Calcutta (The Indian Seamen's Union) and four Labour Unions at Madras were established.
  • The Ahmedabad Textile Labour Association was established on February 25, 1920, by Anasuya Sarabhai (she became president of the association) and Mahatma Gandhi. It emerged following the 1918 Ahmedabad mill strike.
  • All India Trade Union Congress was founded by N.M. Joshi on 31st October, 1920 A.D. at Bombay.
  • Lala Lajpat Rai was the ﬁ rst President of All India Trade Union Congress and Joseph Baptista was the Vice President. Dewan Chaman Lal was its General Secretary (they all were among the founding members) The motive behind its formation was the establishment of International Labour Organization in 1919 A.D. So Indian labourers decided to organize themselves as labour unions. This organization was divided for the ﬁ rst time in 1929 A.D. at Nagpur, Jawaharlal Nehru was president at that time.
  • Communist International was founded in March 1919 A.D. by Vladimir Lenin and Bolshevik Party of Russia.
  • M.N. Roy visited Russia on the invitation of Lenin and joined communist International becoming the ﬁ rst Indian to do so. To stop the growing power and influence of Communist activities, some communist members were arrested on the grounds of a conspiracy held in Kanpur. This case is popularly known as Kanpur Conspiracy.
  • British government in 1924 A.D. started cases against M.N. Roy, Shripad Amrit Dange, Ghulam Hussain, Ramcharan Lal Sharma, Muzaffar Ahmed, Shaukat Usmani, Singaravelu Chettiar, Nalini गुप्त साम्राज्य [Gupta Empire], Shamuddin Hassan, M.R.S. Velayndhun, Dr. Manilal, Sampurnanand and Satyabhakta in Kanpur conspiracy. The government charged them with the conspiracy of establishing a revolutionary organisation in India.
  • Revolutionary period of trade union is believed to be 1926- 1939 A.D.. During this M.N. Roy, Muzaffar Ahmed, Shripad A. Dange, Shauqat Usmani etc. were the main leader of the Trade Union Movement.
  • In 1940 AD, M.N. Roy established Radical Democratic Party .

Non-Cooperation Movement

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Non-Cooperation Movement.

  2. Indian History General Studies B–563 In September, 1920 A.D. Congress met in Calcutta in a special session in which Gandhi proposed the Non-...PCS Pre ’111 paper
  3. The Non-Cooperation Movement was started in –Lower Sub ’081 paper

Non-Cooperation Movement: Core Facts and Sources

  • Moplahs, who had been suffering, tempted to in revolt, a pitched battle between the British regiment and the Moplahs which killed several Europeans. On the other hand, the Khilafat Movement also turned more aggressive, and the situation had become very confusing, and it was very difﬁ cult to make a difference between the meetings of Khilafat Movement and the meeting by Moplah peasants. Thus both the Movements were merged together. असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] Movement
  • Congress held a special session in September 1920 A.D. at Calcutta and passed the resolution of Gandhiji on Non- Cooperation. The resolution was opposed by several senior leaders like C.R. Das and Madan Mohan Malviya . In December 1920 A.D. this resolution was widely supported and C.R. Das moved the resolution for असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] in the Nagpur session.
  • Gandhiji launched असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] movement on 1st August, 1920 A.D. A special session of the Congress was held under the leadership of Lala Lajpat Rai in Calcutta in September, 1920 AD to decide upon the momentous issue of Non- Cooperation. Discussions were held on the Khilafat question, the Government’s failure to protect the innocent people of the Punjab and punish the British of ﬁ cers. The leaders decided that the only way to vindicate the national honour was to establish Swaraj through the policy of Non-Cooperation. The resolution of non-cooperation formally adopted in this session. The resolution of the Calcutta Congress was rati ﬁ ed at the regular session held in Nagpur in December, 1920 AD. The movement got widespread success in western India, Bengal and Northern India. During this movement, Motilal Nehru, Lala Lajpat Rai, Sardar Vallabh Bhai Patel, Jawahar Lal Nehru and Rajendra Prasad boycotted court and jumped into the movement. Gandhiji promised Swaraj within one year of the launching of Non-Cooperation Movement if effectively carried out, at All India Congress Committee. The plan was prepared for a total boycott of Schools, Colleges, Government titles, Government institutions, Foreign goods, etc. This Movement introduced the people with modern techniques of politics and made them hungry for freedom. Communal Unity and massive participation of Muslims was a signiﬁ cant achievements of this movement.
  • The movement destroyed the fear of British power from the hearts of people.
  • At the time when Gandhiji arrived in India, Britain was at World War I. He supported Britain in the war. Gandhiji was given the title of ‘Kaiser-e-Hind’ for his humanitarian work in South Africa, which he returned during the असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] Movement.
  • Jamnalal Bajaj returned the title of Rai Bahadur.
  • Tilak supported the असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] movement but his death on the ﬁ rst day of the movement (1st August, 1920 A.D.) made him unable to see the fruits of the movement. Tilak agreed with the policy of non-cooperation with the British (as in June 1920), but Tilak opposed his method of boycott (especially boycott of legislative councils elutions established by the 1919 Montagu- Chelmsford Reforms).
  • Chauri Chaura incident took place on 4th February, 1922. At a place called Chauri Chaura in Gorakhpur district in United Provinces, police openly ﬁ red at a group of protestors participating in non-cooperation movement, after this, the angered mob burnt down the police station and killed all 23 policemen (3 civilians were also died). This incident is popularly known as the Chauri Chaura incident. After this incident, Gandhiji withdrew the असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] Movement seeing it becoming violent. On 11th and 12th February, 1922 A.D. Congress Working Committee at the Bardoli meeting suspended Non-Cooperation Movement. After the events at Chauri Chaura near Gorakhpur, the Working Committee of the Congress resolves that mass civil disobedience contemplated at Bardoli and elsewhere be suspended and instruct the local Congress Committees forth-with to advise the cultivators to pay the revenue and other dues to the Government and whose payment might have been suspended in anticipation of mass civil disobedience, and instructs them to suspend every other preparatory activity of an offensive nature. The suspension of mass civil disobedience shall be continued till the atmosphere is so nonviolent as to ensure the non-repetition of popular atrocities such as at Gorakhpur or hooliganism such as at Bombay and Madras. On February 24th, 1922 All India Congress Committee organized a meeting in Delhi and a limitation was imposed on all activities that amount to a violation of law. In this session, Dr. Moonje introduced a vote of censure condemning Gandhiji’s decision to call off the movement.
  • Rabindranath Tagore emphasized creative programmes rather than movements and protests by masses. He was against the burning of foreign clothes and wrote a letter to Gandhiji to adopt creative activities instead of mass movements. Rabindranath Tagore called the burning of clothes during the असहयोग आंदोलन [Non-Cooperation Movement] as unwise and he said he was unable to obey the same. Kashi Vidyapith of Varanasi in 1921 A.D., Gujarat Vidyapith at Ahmedabad in 1920 AD and Jamia Millia Islamia at Aligarh in 1920 A.D. which was later shifted to Delhi, were established during the Non-cooperation Movement. Madan Mohan Malviya established Banaras Hindu University in 1916 A.D. and was its Vice-Chancellor till 1939 from 1919 A.D.
  • Indian History General Studies B–563 In September, 1920 A.D. Congress met in Calcutta in a special session in which Gandhi proposed the Non-cooperation resolution which was opposed by C.R. Das. In Dec... UPPSC

Non-Cooperation Movement: Exam Focus and Inscriptions/Details

Chapter 69

Formation of Swaraj Party (1923)

Civil Disobedience Movement: Core Facts and Sources

  • Mahatma Gandhi started his famous दांडी मार्च [Dandi March] accompanied by his trusted volunteers from Sabarmati Ashram on March 12, 1930 A.D. The march was over 241 miles, from Gandhi’s ashram in Sabarmati to Gujarat's coastal village of Dandi. The volunteers walked for 24 days. On April 5, 1930 A.D. he reached Dandi and on 6 April he violated the salt law, manufacturing salt by boiling seawater. This marked the beginning of the सविनय अवज्ञा आंदोलन [Civil Disobedience Movement] Movement. This Movement spread all over India under the leadership of Mahatma Gandhi. A leader like C. Rajagopalachari took up Salt March in Tamil Nadu from Trichinopoly to Vedaranyam.

  • This was the ﬁ rst session where Poorna Swaraj was deﬁ ned.
  • Resolutions on Fundamental Rights and National Economic Policies were drafted by Jawaharlal Nehru with the help of M.N. Roy.
  • Subhas Chandra Bose termed this session as ‘pinnacle of Mahatma Gandhi's popularity and prestige’.

← Chapter 79Second World War →

Chapter 81

Second World War

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Second World War.

Second World War: Core Facts and Sources

  • that is fought for the protection of democracy because India itself is struggling to achieve independence from the British colonial rule.
  • Lord Linlithgow on September 3, 1939 A.D. announced that India will be standing against Germany in the war. Without the authorization of Indian Provincial Ministries, India’s inclusion in the War led to the resignation of all Congress provincial cabinets.
  • Second World War started with Germany attacking Poland on 1st September, 1939 and ended after six years in September 1945.
  • Neville Chamberlain (1937-40) and Winston Churchill (1940-45) were the British Prime Ministers during World War II (1939-1945 AD). Churchill's tenure was from the year 1940 to 1945 and 1951 to 1955.

Chapter 84

Cripps Mission (1942)

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Cripps Mission (1942).

UPPSC asks most here

  1. After reaching India in March, 1942 A.D., Stafford Cripps presented the proposal whose major recommendations were- i- After war, India shoul...Lower Sub ’981 paper

Cripps Mission (1942): Core Facts and Sources

  • Code - (a) (A) is false but (R) is true. (b) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not correct explanation of (A). (c) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is correct explanation of (A). (d) (A) is true but (R) is false. U.P. R.O./A.R.O. (Pre) 2023 Ans. (c) After refusing August offer completely, Gandhiji on 17 October, 1940 A.D. called for limited Satyagrah (Individual Satyagrah) by few selected individuals. The limited individual Satyagrah had two aims - to show the displeasure of people of India with British India's participation in war and to give another chance to British government to accept demands of Indian people. Gandhiji and Congress, did not want to disrupt war efforts of British by starting mass movement due to their anti-Nazi stand. Cripps Mission (1942) Considering the rising power of Japan, international community, especially United States of America, China and Australia pressured Britain for political solution settlement with India. Due to urgent need for British action to solve the political problem, Stafford Cripps arrived in India in March, 1942 A.D. to negotiate with Indian politicians. After reaching India in March, 1942 A.D., Stafford Cripps presented a proposal whose major recommendations were: i- After Second World War India should be given a Dominion Status which is consistent with the declaration (i.e. associated by common allegiance to the crown, while not subordinate in domestic/external affairs). ii- Indians should be given the right to form their own Constitution for which the elected constitution making body will be initiated after the War. iii- Any provinces not agreeing to the new Constitution would have the right to keep themselves out of the proposed Union. and to retain its present constitutional position.
  • Mahatma Gandhi termed this deal as a post-dated cheque on a falling bank.
  • Pt. Jawaharlal Nehru and Maulana Azad were the of ﬁ cial negotiators from the Congress side in talks with Cripps Mission.

Chapter 87

Cabinet Mission Plan (1946)

Key historical facts, chronology, and UPPSC questions on Cabinet Mission Plan (1946).

UPPSC asks most here

  1. Cabinet Mission, 1946 comprised of three cabinet ministers. Who among the following was not its member?Lower Sub ’02; PCS Pre ’942 papers
  2. The ‘Cabinet-Mission’ of 1946 was led by:PCS Pre ’151 paper
  3. Who amongst the following headed the 1946 Cabinet Mission?PCS Mains ’151 paper

Cabinet Mission Plan (1946): Core Facts and Sources

  • कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] Plan (1946)
  • Clement Attlee became British Prime Minister on 26 th July, 1945 A.D. British Prime Minister Clement Attlee announced on February 19 th, 1946 A.D., the dispatch of the कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] for constitutional reforms. Cabinet Mission arrived in Delhi on 24th March 1946 A.D. The Cabinet Mission to India consisted Lord Pethick Lawrence was secretory of State for India Sir Stafford Cripps the President of the Board of Trade and A.V . Alexander, First Lord of the Admiralty. They arrived on March 24, 1946 A.D. and held extensive discussions in April with almost all major leaders of the country. The Cabinet Mission proposed a three-tier polity for India. The major recommendation of the Cabinet Mission was to divide the provinces into three sections i.e. A, B and C.
  • On 16 th May, 1946 A.D. कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] announced its recommendations and its main proposals were- (1) There should be a Union of India, embracing both British India and the states, which should deal with the following subjects – foreign affairs, defence and communications; of India would be given Independence. (2) The Muslim League’s demand for Pakistan was rejected. (3) The Union should have an Executive and Legislature constituted from British India Province and Princely State Representatives. (4) A संविधान सभा [Constituent Assembly] would be formed with the representatives of Provincial Legislative Assembly Chief Commissioners Provinces and the Princely States.
  • कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] also recommended reorganization of Viceroy’s Executive Council and formation of the interim government in which all the ministerial posts including war member would be held by Indians.
  • Gandhiji supported कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] and said this proposal is the best document the British Government could have produced in the circumstances, while cautioning that their best may possible be harmful. Maulana Abul Kalam Azad was the Congress President during the arrival of Cabinet Mission.
  • The ‘Cabinet-Mission’ of 1946 was led by: → Sir Pethick-Lawrence. UPPSC
  • Who amongst the following headed the 1946 कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission]? → Sir Pethick-Lawrence. UPPSC
  • Who arrived India, in 1946 after Second World War? → कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission]. UPPSC
  • The कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] arrived in India on March 24, 1946 A.D. and held extensive discussions in April with almost all major leaders of the country. The Cabinet Mission proposed a three-tier polity for I... UPPSC
  • कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission], 1946 comprised of three cabinet ministers. Who among the following was not its member? → Lord Emery. UPPSC ×2

Cabinet Mission Plan (1946): Exam Focus and Inscriptions/Details

  • The कैबिनेट मिशन [Cabinet Mission] of 1946 AD came to India to discuss the transfer of power from the British Government to the Indian Leadership, with the aim of preserving India's unity and granting it independenc... UPPSC

Constitutional Development of India: Core Facts and Sources

  • In the year 1929 AD, at the annual session of Congress held at Lahore, ‘Purna Swaraj’ or Complete Independence was accepted as the goal of the Congress. On the banks of the river Ravi, at midnight on 31 st December, 1929 AD, the tricolour ﬂ ag of Indian Independence was unfurled amidst cheer and jubilation. 26 th January, 1930 AD was marked as the ﬁ rst Independence Day, which was to be so celebrated every year. To commemorate this historic day, it was decided to enforce the Constitution on 26th January, 1950 AD.

Modern History: Miscellaneous: Core Facts and Sources

  • Modern History : Miscellaneous
  • In 19th century the fear of Russian expansion towards India became the basis of Anglo-Afghan relationships. The Treaty of Turkmenchay (1828 AD) created fear among the British due to the increasing inﬂ uence of Russians. Experts in England called this ‘The Crisis in India.”
  • North Western passes were the key to entering India. Then it was thought that Afghanistan should be under a friendly government or a government which is in congruence with Britishers. The British "Forward Policy" in 19 th century Afghanistan was a proactive, aggressive strategy designed to establish a buffer state against Russian expansion towards India, particularly during the ﬁ rst Anglo-Afghan War (1839- 42). It was advocated by Lord Auckland. This became the basis of Anglo-Afghan relations.
  • In 19th century Odisha was hit by a severe famine in 1866-67 AD. An estimated one million deaths took place in Odisha. This famine was described as ‘Sea of Calamity’. Odisha famine emerged decisively in the history of Indian famines because after this a Famine Commission under George Campbell was constituted to look into Famines.
  • Lord Hastings suppressed the Pindaris. Pindaris were ﬁ rst mentioned at the time of the मुगल साम्राज्य [Mughal Empire] attack on Maharashtra in 1689 AD. In the beginning of 19th century, Pindaris had three main leaders - Chittu, Wasil Muhammad and Karim Khan.
  • Gandhi and Tilak both toured throughout India and worked for social upliftment. Tilak was the ﬁ rst nationalist leader who worked for establishing close relations with masses at the root level.
  • G.V . Mavalankar who became the last President of Central Legislative Assembly in 1946 AD was the ﬁ rst speaker to enter the ofﬁ ce wearing a Gandhi cap and not the ‘wig’.
  • Famous constitutionalist B.R. Ambedkar was born on 14 April, 1891 AD at Mhow (now Dr. Ambedkar Nagar) in M.P. and died on 6 December 1956 AD. He was awarded Bharat Ratna posthumously.
  • Rabindranath Tagore, also known as Gurudev won a Nobel Prize in literature and was a famous author. He was born on 7 May 1861 AD at Calcutta as son of Debendranath Tagore and Sharada Devi. In 1901 AD, he started a school at Shantiniketan which was later converted into a University named as Vishva Bharti in 1921 AD. In 1913 A.D. Tagore was awarded with Nobel Prize for literature for his literacy works like - 'Song Offerings', the English translation of his collection of poems ‘Gitanjali’. He was called as the Great sentinel by Gandhiji. Gandhiji believed that Rabindranath Tagore emphasized on moral forces for the freedom struggle.
  • The famous slogan of Jai Jawan Jai Kisan was given by former P.M. of India Lal Bahadur Shastri .
